(4)

B
Additional Background for
Section

2

Proposition B.1
.

In an integral quantale
V
, for all
a,b\in V
,
a\oplus b\sqsubseteq a\sqcap b
.

Proof.

We first prove that in any quantale
\oplus
is monotone in both arguments:

\displaystyle x\sqsubseteq y

\displaystyle\iff x\sqcup y=y

\displaystyle\implies a\oplus(x\sqcup y)=a\oplus y

\displaystyle\iff(a\oplus x)\sqcup(a\oplus y)=(a\oplus y)

(Join distributivity)

\displaystyle\iff(a\oplus x)\sqsubseteq(a\oplus y)

(5)

Second, we prove that, in any integral quantale,
a\oplus b\sqsubseteq a
:

\displaystyle b\sqsubseteq\top

\displaystyle\implies a\oplus b\sqsubseteq a\oplus\top

(by
5
)

\displaystyle\iff a\oplus b\sqsubseteq a\oplus k

(
k=\top
)

\displaystyle\iff a\oplus b\sqsubseteq a

Similarly, we have
a\oplus b\sqsubseteq b
, and thus
a\oplus b\sqsubseteq a\sqcap b

∎

Enriched Categories

We only gloss over the technical details of enriched categories in the body of the paper, so we take some time here to formalise our discussion. Let us first recall the main definitions concerning enriched categories. The standard reference is
[
42
]
. We fix an arbitrary monoidal category
\mathcal{V}
. We denote its monoidal product
\boxtimes\colon\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
, its monoidal unit
I
, its associators
\alpha_{a,b,c}\colon(a\boxtimes b)\boxtimes c\to a\boxtimes(b\boxtimes c)
, its left unitors
l_{a}\colon I\boxtimes a\to a
, and its right unitors
r_{a}\colon a\boxtimes I\to a
.

Definition B.2
.

A small
\mathcal{V}
-enriched category

\mathcal{C}
(or simply
\mathcal{V}
-category
) consists of:

•

A set
\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
called the set of objects;

•

For each pair
(a,b)
of objects in
\mathcal{C}
, an object
\mathcal{C}(a,b)\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{V})
called the hom-object;

•

For each triple
(a,b,c)
of objects in
\mathcal{C}
, a morphism
M_{a,b,c}\colon\mathcal{C}(b,c)\boxtimes\mathcal{C}(a,b)\to\mathcal{C}(a,c)
in
\mathcal{V}
called composition;

•

For each object
a
in
\mathcal{C}
, a morphism
j_{a}\colon I\to\mathcal{C}(a,a)
in
\mathcal{V}
called the identity;

They satisfy the following coherence conditions for all
a,b,c,d\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
:

\displaystyle M_{a,c,d}\circ\left(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}(c,d)}\boxtimes M_{a,b,c}\right)\circ\alpha_{\mathcal{C}(c,d),\mathcal{C}(b,c),\mathcal{C}(a,b)}

\displaystyle=M_{a,b,d}\circ\left(M_{b,c,d}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}(a,b)}\right)

(6)

\displaystyle M_{a,b,b}\circ\left(j_{b}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}(a,b)}\right)

\displaystyle=l_{\mathcal{C}(a,b)}

(7)

\displaystyle M_{a,a,b}\circ\left(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}(a,b)}\boxtimes j_{a}\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathcal{C}(a,b)}.

(8)

Definition B.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
be small
\mathcal{V}
-enriched categories. A
\mathcal{V}
-enriched functor
(also called
\mathcal{V}
-functor
)
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
consists of a function
F_{0}\colon\mathop{Ob}(C)\to\mathop{Ob}(D)
and an
\left(\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\right)
-indexed collection of morphisms of
\mathcal{V}
, written
F_{a,b}\colon\mathcal{C}(a,b)\to\mathcal{D}(F_{0}(a),F_{0}(b))
, such that for all
a,b,c\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
:

\displaystyle F_{a,c}\circ M_{a,b,c}

\displaystyle=M_{F_{0}(a),F_{0}(b),F_{0}(c)}\circ\left(F_{b,c}\boxtimes F_{a,c}\right)

(9)

\displaystyle j_{F_{0}(a)}

\displaystyle=F_{a,a}\circ j_{a}.

(10)

Example B.4
.

For
\mathcal{V}=(\mathsf{Set},\times,1)
, the definitions above describe the usual small categories and functors between them. In many applications,
\mathcal{V}
is a category of sets equipped with structure (e.g. groups, metric spaces, etc.). In such cases,
\mathcal{V}
-categories are small categories whose hom-sets have extra structure preserved by composition, and
\mathcal{V}
-functors are functors that locally (i.e. on each hom-set) preserve this structure.

Remark B.5
.

Monoidal categories are defined in ordinary category theory using the data of a bifunctor of type
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
called the monoidal product that obeys certain coherence conditions. Lifting this concept to enriched categories requires the construction of an enriched category
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
from an enriched category
\mathcal{C}
. When
\mathcal{V}
is a symmetric monoidal category, we can use the tensor product of
\mathcal{V}
-categories defined in
[
42
, Section 1.4]
recalled below.

Definition B.6
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a symmetric monoidal category and let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-category. The
\mathcal{V}
-category
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
is defined as follows:

•

The objects of
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
are pairs of objects of
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.
\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})=\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
.

•

For each pair
\left((a,a^{\prime}),(b,b^{\prime})\right)\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})\times\mathop{Ob}\left(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\right)
, we define
(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})((a,a^{\prime}),(b,b^{\prime}))\coloneqq\mathcal{C}(a,b)\boxtimes\mathcal{C}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
.

•

Let
(a,a^{\prime}),(b,b^{\prime}),(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathop{Ob}(C\times C)
. The composition morphism
M_{(a,a^{\prime}),(b,b^{\prime}),(c,c^{\prime})}
is given by the following composite, where
\cong
denotes an isomorphism constructed using the symmetric monoidal structure of
\mathcal{V}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})((b,b^{\prime}),(c,c^{\prime}))\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})((a,a^{\prime}),(b,b^{\prime}))\\
=\mathcal{C}(b,c)\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}(a,b)\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(b,c)\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}(a,b)\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{C}(a,c)\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}(a^{\prime},c^{\prime})\\
=(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})((a,a^{\prime}),(c,c^{\prime}))\end{array}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{a,b,c}\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}M_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}}}

•

For each
(a,a^{\prime})\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})
we define
j_{(a,a^{\prime})}
as the following composite.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{\mathcal{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{\mathcal{V}}\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}I_{\mathcal{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{C}(a,a)\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime})=(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})((a,a^{\prime}),(a,a^{\prime}))\end{array}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{a}\otimes_{\mathcal{V}}j_{a^{\prime}}}

{V}\mathsf{HMet}
Enrichment

We carefully develop our discussion on the characterisation of
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-enriched categories and functors.

Lemma B.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category such that for each
a,b\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
there is a
V
-hemimetric space
(\mathcal{C}(a,b),d_{a,b})
. If for all
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(a,b)
and
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(b,c)
,

(d_{b,c}\boxtimes d_{a,b})((g,f),(g^{\prime},f^{\prime}))\sqsubseteq d_{a,c}(g\circ f,g^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}),

(11)

then there is a
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-category with underlying category
\mathcal{C}
. The same result holds for
{V}\mathsf{PMet}
.

Proof.

Let
M_{a,b,c}\colon\mathcal{C}(b,c)\boxtimes\mathcal{C}(a,c)\to\mathcal{C}(a,c)
be the composition morphism defined by
M_{a,b,c}(g,f)\coloneqq g\circ f
for any
f\in\mathcal{C}(a,b)
and
g\in\mathcal{C}(b,c)
. Note that
11
ensures that
M_{a,b,c}
is a nonexpansive map, i.e. a morphism in
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
. For any
a\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
, let
j_{a}\colon 1_{\boxtimes}\to\mathcal{C}(a,a)
be defined by
j_{a}(\bullet)\coloneqq\mathrm{id}_{a}
. We have
\top(\bullet,\bullet)=\top=k\sqsubseteq d_{a,a}(\mathrm{id}_{a},\mathrm{id}_{a})
because
d_{a,a}
satisfies reflexivity (and
V
is integral), thus
j_{a}
is nonexpansive as well. It remains to check the coherence conditions of
Definition

B.2
.

•

For
6
, we can explicitly compute how both sides act on
\mathcal{C}(c,d)\boxtimes\mathcal{C}(b,c)\boxtimes\mathcal{C}(a,b)
. They send
(h,g,f)
to
(h\circ g)\circ f
and
h\circ(g\circ f)
which are equal because
\mathcal{C}
is a category so its composition is associative.

•

For
7
, both sides act on
1_{\boxtimes}\boxtimes\mathcal{C}(a,b)
by sending
(\bullet,f)
to
\mathrm{id}_{b}\circ f
and
f
which are equal because
\mathcal{C}
is a category so the identity morphism is neutral for composition.

•

The proof of
8
is symmetric to the previous point.

By construction,
\mathcal{C}
is the underlying category of the resulting
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-category.
∎

Lemma B.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-categories and
F\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be a functor between their underlying categories. If
F
is locally nonexpansive, namely, the assignment
f\mapsto Ff
is a nonexpansive map
\mathcal{C}(a,b)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(Fa,Fb)
for all
a,b\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
, then
F
is the underlying functor of a
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-functor.

Proof.

Define
F_{0}\colon\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{D})
by
F_{0}(a)\coloneqq Fa
for all
a\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
. Similarly, for any
a,b\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
and a morphism
f\in\mathcal{C}(a,b)
, we define
F_{a,b}(f)\coloneqq Ff
. Since
F
is locally nonexpansive,
F_{a,b}
is indeed a morphism
\mathcal{C}(a,b)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(F_{0}a,F_{0}b)
in
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
. The coherence conditions
9
and
10
are satisfied because
F
is a functor between the underlying categories of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
(explicit checks can be made as in the proof of
Lemma

B.8
). By construction,
F
is the underlying functor of the resulting
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-functor.
∎

Corollary B.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-categories and
F\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be an isomorphism between their underlying categories. If
F
is locally an isometry, namely, the assignment
f\mapsto Ff
is an isometry
\mathcal{C}(a,b)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(Fa,Fb)
for all
a,b\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
, then
F
is an enriched isomorphism, namely, both
F
and
F^{-1}
are
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-functors.

Proof.

We know by
Lemma

B.8
that since
F
is locally nonexpansive, it is a
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-functor. Let
F^{-1}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
be the inverse of the functor
F
. For any
f,g\in\mathcal{D}(a,b)
, since
F
is locally an isometry, we have

d_{F^{-1}a,F^{-1}b}(F^{-1}f,F^{-1}F)=d_{a,b}(FF^{-1}f,FF^{-1}g)=d_{a,b}(f,g),

hence
F^{-1}
is locally nonexpansive (it is even locally an isometry). Therefore, by
Lemma

B.8
again,
F^{-1}
is a
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-functor, and it is straightforward to check that it is the inverse of
F
(the
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-functors have the same action as their underlying functors).
∎

Corollary B.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-category, whose underlying category is equipped with a strict monoidal product
\otimes\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
. Then,
\mathcal{C}
is a strict
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-enriched monoidal category if and only if for all
a,b,c,d\in\mathop{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
,
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(a,b)
,
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(c,d)
,

(d_{a,b}\boxtimes d_{c,d})((f,f^{\prime}),(g,g^{\prime}))\sqsubseteq d_{a\otimes c,b\otimes d}(f\otimes g,f^{\prime}\otimes g^{\prime}).

(12)

The same result holds for
{V}\mathsf{PMet}
.

Proof.

We simply need to show that the monoidal product bifunctor is enriched. We make
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
into a
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-category using
Definition

B.6
. The hemimetric on hom-sets of
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
is defined in terms of distances on hom-sets of
\mathcal{C}
, namely
d_{(a,c),(b,d)}=d_{a,b}\boxtimes d_{c,d}
. Then,
12
precisely says that
\otimes
is locally nonexpansive. Thus, it is a
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
-functor by
Lemma

B.8
.
∎

C
Proofs of
Section

3

Recall the definition of the
syntactic distance

d^{\mathcal{U}}
.
Let
\mathcal{U}=(\Sigma,E,E_{q})
be a
V
-quantitative monoidal theory. For any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Sigma
-terms
f,g\colon n\rightarrow m
, let the distance from
f
to
g
be given by

d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,g)\coloneqq\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon\mid f=_{\varepsilon}g\in\overline{E_{q}}\}.

(13)

Proof of
Lemma

16
.

Let us first prove the reflexivity and triangle inequality on
\Sigma
-terms.

•

For any
\Sigma
-terms
f\colon n\rightarrow m
, we know that
f=f
is provable from
E
, thus
f=_{\top}f\in\overline{E_{q}}
by
Refl
. Hence,

k=\top=\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon\mid f=_{\varepsilon}f\in\overline{E_{q}}\}=d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,f).

•

For any
\Sigma
-terms
f,g,h\colon n\rightarrow m
, we have the following derivation.

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}

\displaystyle(f,g)\oplus d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(g,h)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon\mid f=_{\varepsilon}g\in\overline{E_{q}}\}\oplus\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon^{\prime}\mid g=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}h\in\overline{E_{q}}\}

\displaystyle(\textrm{def.~of}\,d^{\mathcal{U}}\,)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon\oplus\varepsilon^{\prime}\mid f=_{\varepsilon}g,\,g=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}h\in\overline{E_{q}}\}

\displaystyle(\textrm{join-continuity})

On the other hand, we recall the definition of
d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,h)
.

d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,h)\coloneqq\bigsqcup\{\vartheta\mid f=_{\vartheta}h\in\overline{E_{q}}\}

Now, by
Triang
, we have that if
f=_{\varepsilon}g,\,g=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}h\in\overline{E_{q}}
, then
f=_{\varepsilon\oplus\varepsilon^{\prime}}h\in\overline{E_{q}}
. Hence, for any such
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
,

\varepsilon\oplus\varepsilon^{\prime}\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,h).

We conclude the desired inequality holds:

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,g)

\displaystyle\oplus d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(g,h)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon\oplus\varepsilon^{\prime}\mid f=_{\varepsilon}g,\,g=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}h\in\overline{E_{q}}\}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,h).

We can now show that
d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}
is a well-defined function on
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(n,m)\times\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(n,m)
. Namely, if
f=f^{\prime}
and
g=g^{\prime}
are provable from
E
, then
d^{\mathcal{U}}(f,g)=d^{\mathcal{U}}(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})
. By the triangle inequality, we have

d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,f^{\prime})\oplus d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\oplus d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(g^{\prime},g)\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,g).

But by
Refl
, we also have that
f=_{\top}f^{\prime},g=_{\top}g^{\prime}\in\overline{E_{q}}
, so
d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,f^{\prime})=d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(g^{\prime},g)=\top=k
. Thus, we obtain
d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,g)
and the symmetric inequation is proven similarly. It follows that
d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}
is a
V
-hemimetric on
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(n,m)
.

Moreover, if
\overline{E_{q}}
is closed under
Symm
, then for any
\Sigma
-terms
f,g\colon n\rightarrow m
,

f=_{\varepsilon}g\in\overline{E_{q}}\,\Longleftrightarrow\,g=_{\varepsilon}f\in\overline{E_{q}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon\mid f=_{\varepsilon}g\in\overline{E_{q}}\}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon\mid g=_{\varepsilon}f\in\overline{E_{q}}\}=d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(g,f),

and
d^{\mathcal{U}}
is indeed a
V
-pseudometric.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

17
.

If we explicitly compute the left-hand side similarly to the triangle inequality part of
Lemma

16
, we get the following.

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f_{0},f_{1})\ast

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{U}}_{m,\ell}(g_{0},g_{1})

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon\ast\varepsilon^{\prime}\mid g_{0}=_{\varepsilon}g_{1},\,f_{0}=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\overline{E_{q}}}f_{1}\},

where
\ast
is one of
\{\oplus,\sqcap\}
.
Now, by
\textnormal{{Seq}}_{\oplus}
or
\textnormal{{Seq}}_{\sqcap}
, we know that if
g_{0}=_{\varepsilon}g_{1},\,f_{0}=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}f_{1}\in\overline{E_{q}}
, then
g_{0}\circ f_{0}=_{\varepsilon\ast\varepsilon^{\prime}}g_{1}\circ f_{1}\in\overline{E_{q}}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f_{0},f_{1})\ast

\displaystyle d^{\mathcal{U}}_{m,\ell}(g_{0},g_{1})

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon\ast\varepsilon^{\prime}\mid f_{0}=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}f_{1},\,g_{0}=_{\varepsilon}g_{1}\in\overline{E_{q}}\}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup\{\vartheta\mid f_{0};g_{0}=_{\vartheta}f_{1};g_{1}\in\overline{E_{q}}\}

\displaystyle=d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,\ell}(f_{0};g_{0}\,,\,f_{1};g_{1}).\qed

Proof of
Proposition

19
.

This is a simple application of
Lemmas

B.7
and
B.10
combined with
Lemmas

17
and
18
. Let us make an example with
E_{q}^{\mathsf{P}\oplus\sqcap}
, the closure we will need in
Section

5
. Looking at its row in
Table

C.1
, we need to show
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}
is
{V}\mathsf{PMet}_{\oplus}
-enriched monoidal.

Since
\overline{E_{q}}
is closed under
\textnormal{{Seq}}_{\oplus}
,
Lemma

17
says that for any
f_{0},f_{1}\colon n\rightarrow m
and
g_{0},g_{1}\colon m\rightarrow\ell
,

d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f_{0},f_{1})\oplus d^{\mathcal{U}}_{m,\ell}(g_{0},g_{1})\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,\ell}(f_{0};g_{0}\,,\,f_{1};g_{1}).

This implies that sequential composition is a nonexpansive map of type

{;}\colon\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(n,m)\boxtimes_{\oplus}\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(m,\ell)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(n,\ell).

Therefore, we conclude by
Lemma

B.7
that
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}
is
{V}\mathsf{PMet}_{\oplus}
-enriched.

Next, by closure under
\textnormal{{Par}}_{\sqcap}
,
Lemma

18
says that for any
f_{0},f_{1}\colon n\rightarrow n^{\prime}
and
f_{1},g_{1}\colon m\rightarrow m^{\prime}
,

d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,n^{\prime}}(f_{0},f_{1})\sqcap d^{\mathcal{U}}_{m,m^{\prime}}(g_{0},g_{1})\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n+m,n^{\prime}+m^{\prime}}(f_{0}\otimes g_{0},f_{1}\otimes g_{1}).

(14)

By
Proposition

B.1
and transitivity of
\sqsubseteq
, we obtain

d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,n^{\prime}}(f_{0},f_{1})\oplus d^{\mathcal{U}}_{m,m^{\prime}}(g_{0},g_{1})\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n+m,n^{\prime}+m^{\prime}}(f_{0}\otimes g_{0},f_{1}\otimes g_{1}),

so parallel composition is a nonexpansive map of type

{\otimes}\colon\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(n,n^{\prime})\boxtimes_{\oplus}\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(m,m^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(n+n^{\prime},m+m^{\prime}).

(15)

Therefore, we conclude by
Corollary

B.10
that
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}
is
{V}\mathsf{PMet}_{\oplus}
-enriched monoidal.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

23
.

By
Lemma

B.8
, to show that
M
is enriched, it suffices to show that for any
\Sigma
-terms
f,g
,

d^{\mathcal{U}}(f,g)\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{C}}(Mf,Mg).

By definition of
d^{\mathcal{U}}

13
, this inequality holds if and only if for any
\varepsilon
such that
f=_{\varepsilon}g\in\overline{E_{q}}
,
\varepsilon\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{C}}(Mf,Mg)
. Equivalently, we need to show that all of
\overline{E_{q}}
is true in
M
. This readily follows from the fact that all of
E_{q}
is true in
M
, and all the inference rules used to generate
\overline{E_{q}}
are valid in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

24
.

Assume that
f=_{\varepsilon}g
is true in all
\overline{E_{q}}
-models of the theory
\mathcal{U}
. Observe that the canonical identity functor
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}\to\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}
can be extended to an enriched functor of type
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}\to\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}
, which is a
\overline{E_{q}}
-model of
\mathcal{U}
. By
Lemma

B.8
, we have that
\varepsilon\sqsubseteq d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,g)
. Recall that

d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,g)=\bigsqcup\{\varepsilon^{\prime}\mid f=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}g\in\overline{E_{q}}\}.

Using
Join
, we can deduce that
f=_{d^{\mathcal{U}}_{n,m}(f,g)}g\in\overline{E_{q}}
.
Finally, using
Mon
, we can derive
f=_{\varepsilon}g\in\overline{E_{q}}
. ∎

Remark C.1
.

We proved that
14
implies nonexpansiveness of
15
, but the former is strictly stronger than the latter. Indeed,
14
is equivalent to parallel composition being a nonexpansive map of type

\scalebox{0.91}{${\otimes}\colon\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(n,n^{\prime})\boxtimes_{\sqcap}\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(m,m^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}(n+n^{\prime},m+m^{\prime})$}.

(16)

Equivalently, it says that
\textnormal{{Par}}_{\sqcap}
is valid.

D
Proofs of
Section

4

Proof of
Lemma

27
.

Given
f\colon n\rightarrow m
, Item 2 gives no choice on the scalars
f_{ij}
, they must be the entries of the matrix
F(f)
.
By definition,
F
sends the tensoring
\bigotimes_{i\in\underline{\mathbf{m}},j\in\underline{\mathbf{n}}}f_{ij}
to a diagonal matrix containing all the scalars in some fixed order
f_{11},f_{21},\dots f_{m1},f_{12},\dots,f_{mn}
.
We define
w^{n}_{m}
and
b^{n}_{m}
as the preimages of the following matrices:

F(w^{n}_{m})=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{m}&\overset{n}{\cdots}&\mathbf{I}_{m}\end{bmatrix}\qquad F(b^{n}_{m})=\begin{bmatrix}1_{R}\\
\vdots\\
1_{R}\end{bmatrix}\oplus\overset{n}{\cdots}\oplus\begin{bmatrix}1_{R}\\
\vdots\\
1_{R}\end{bmatrix}.

One can verify (by direct computation) that the
(i,j)
-entry of the matrix
F(b^{n}_{m});F\left(\bigotimes_{i\in\underline{\mathbf{m}},j\in\underline{\mathbf{n}}}f_{ij}\right);F(w^{n}_{m})
is
f_{ij}
. Therefore, applying
F
to both sides of the equation in Item 1 yields the same matrix. Since
F
is fully faithful by
Proposition

26
, Item 1 must hold.
∎

We restate the definition of the entrywise preorder for future reference:

A\leq B\text{ if and only if, for all }i,j,\ A_{ij}\leq B_{ij}\text{ in }R.

(17)

Proof of
Theorem

29
.

As the enrichment is given by the preorder
17
, all we need to check is that both composition and the monoidal product in
\mathsf{Mat}_{R}
are order-preserving, then apply
Lemma

B.7
and
Corollary

B.10
.

For composition, this boils down to showing that, for any matrices
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Mat}_{R}(n,m)
and
B,B^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Mat}_{R}(m,t)
, if
(B,A)\leq(B^{\prime},A^{\prime})
then
BA\leq B^{\prime}A^{\prime}
. Let us look at the
(i,j)
-entry of
BA
.

\displaystyle(BA)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{k=1}^{m}B_{ik}A_{kj}

\displaystyle\leq\Sigma_{k=1}^{m}B^{\prime}_{ik}A^{\prime}_{kj}

\displaystyle=(B^{\prime}A^{\prime})_{ij}

The inequality holds by
Assumption

28
using that
(B_{ik},A_{kj})\leq(B^{\prime}_{ik},A^{\prime}_{kj})
implies
A_{kj}\leq A^{\prime}_{kj}
and
B_{ik}\leq B^{\prime}_{ik}
. Therefore, we get
B_{ik}A_{kj}\leq B^{\prime}_{ik}A^{\prime}_{kj}
.

For the monoidal product, we need to check that, for any matrices
N,N^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Mat}_{R}(n,n^{\prime})
and
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Mat}_{R}(m,m^{\prime})
, if
(N,M)\leq(N^{\prime},M^{\prime})
, then
N\oplus M\leq N^{\prime}\oplus M^{\prime}
. Since
\leq
is checked entrywise according to
17
, this follows directly by definition of
\oplus
.
∎

E
Probability Distributions and the Distribution Monad

Definition E.1

(Probability distributions)
.

Given a set
X
, a
(probability) distribution
on
X
is a function
\varphi\colon X\rightarrow[0,1]
satisfying
\sum_{x\in X}\varphi(x)=1
. We call
\varphi(x)
the
weight
of
\varphi
at
x
, and we write
\varphi(S)
for the weight of
\varphi
on
S\subseteq X
, namely,
\varphi(S)=\sum_{x\in S}\varphi(x)
. The
support
of
\varphi
is the subset
\mathrm{supp}(\varphi)\subseteq X
containing all elements where
\varphi
assigns nonzero weight. In the sequel, distributions are assumed to have a finite support.

We denote with
\mathscr{D}X
the set of finitely supported distributions on
X
, formally,

\mathscr{D}X=\{\varphi\colon X\rightarrow[0,1]\mid\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}$}\varphi(x)=1\text{ and }|\mathrm{supp}(\varphi)|<\infty\}.

For any function
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
,
\mathscr{D}f\colon\mathscr{D}X\rightarrow\mathscr{D}Y
denotes the
pushforward
map, it sends a distribution
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}X
to the distribution
\mathscr{D}f(\varphi)\colon Y\rightarrow[0,1]
defined by

\mathscr{D}f(\varphi)(y)=\varphi(f^{-1}(y))=\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}\varphi(x).

In words, the weight of
\mathscr{D}f(\varphi)
at
y\in Y
is the total weight of
\varphi
on the preimage of
y
under
f
. This yields a functor
\mathscr{D}\colon\mathsf{Set}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
.

The functor
\mathscr{D}\colon\mathsf{Set}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
has a monad structure. We will not need the details here, but it is useful to recall the notion of convex combination, which is part of the algebraic theory of the monad. For any
p\in[0,1]
and
\varphi,\psi\in\mathscr{D}X
, their
convex combination

\varphi+_{p}\psi
is a distribution over
X
defined by

(\varphi+_{p}\psi)(x)=p\varphi(x)+(1-p)\psi(x).

The Kleisli category of the monad
\mathscr{D}\colon\mathsf{Set}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
is often seen as a category of (discrete) stochastic processes (sometimes also called Markov kernels
[
30
]
). We will study its full subcategory containing only finite sets
\underline{\mathbf{n}}=\{0,\dots,n-1\}
as objects, which may be defined concretely as follows. Very concisely,
\mathsf{FStoch}
is the wide symmetric monoidal subcategory of
\mathsf{Mat}_{[0,1]}
containing all and only the matrices that are
stochastic
, namely, whose columns all sum up to
1
. We report a direct definition of
\mathsf{FStoch}
below, expanding the one provided in the main text as a subcategory of
\mathsf{Mat}_{[0,1]}
.

Definition E.2

(
\mathsf{FStoch}
)
.

The symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{FStoch}
has objects the natural numbers, and a morphism
n\rightarrow m
is an
m\times n

stochastic matrix
, i.e. a matrix with entries in the interval
[0,1]
such that the sum of the entries in a column always equals
1
. The identity maps are identity matrices, and composition is computed by matrix multiplication, namely, if
A\colon n\rightarrow m
and
A^{\prime}\colon m\rightarrow\ell
are two stochastic matrices, then
A;A^{\prime}\colon n\rightarrow\ell
is defined as
A^{\prime}A
. When
n
is
0
, there is a unique morphism
0\rightarrow m
that we identify as the empty
m\times 0
matrix denoted by
[]
. When
m=0
, there are no morphisms
n\rightarrow 0
.
The monoidal product is given by addition on objects and by direct sum on matrices: given
A\colon n\rightsquigarrow n^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}\colon m\rightsquigarrow m^{\prime}
,
A\oplus A^{\prime}\colon(m+m^{\prime})\times(n+n^{\prime})
is defined as
A\oplus A^{\prime}\coloneqq\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\scriptstyle A&\scriptstyle\mathbf{0}\\
\scriptstyle\mathbf{0}&\scriptstyle A^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. For each
n,m
, the symmetry
\sigma_{n,m}
is
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\scriptstyle\mathbf{0}&\scriptstyle\mathbf{I}_{n}\\
\scriptstyle\mathbf{I}_{m}&\scriptstyle\mathbf{0}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
.

F
Proofs of
Section

5

The proof of
Theorem

37
relies on nonexpansiveness of sequential and parallel composition in
\mathsf{FStoch}
with the chosen monoidal product being
\boxtimes_{+}
. These facts are encapsulated by the following two lemmas.

Lemma F.1
.

For any matrices
A,B\in\mathsf{FStoch}(n,m)
and
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FStoch}(m,\ell)
,

\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A;A^{\prime},B;B^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A^{\prime},A)+\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(B^{\prime},B).

Lemma F.2
.

For any matrices
A,B\in\mathsf{FStoch}(n,m)
and
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FStoch}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})
,

\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A\oplus A^{\prime},B\oplus B^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A,B)+\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}).

In our proof of
Lemma

F.1
we will make use of an equivalent definition of
\mathsf{tv}
, which relies on the notion of coupling. We recall it below.

Definition F.3

(Couplings)
.

Given two distributions
\varphi,\psi\in\mathscr{D}X
, a
coupling
of
\varphi
and
\psi
is a distribution in
\mathscr{D}(X\times X)
such that, for any
x\in X
, the total weight on
\{x\}\times X
is
\varphi(x)
and the total weight on
X\times\{x\}
is
\psi(x)
. More concisely, the set of couplings of
\varphi
and
\psi
can be defined as

\mathfrak{C}(\varphi,\psi)=\left\{\omega\in\mathscr{D}(X\times X)\mid\mathscr{D}\pi_{1}(\omega)=\varphi,\mathscr{D}\pi_{2}(\omega)=\psi\right\},

where
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\colon X\times X\rightarrow X
are the projections. We also call
\mathscr{D}\pi_{1}(\omega)
and
\mathscr{D}\pi_{2}(\omega)
the
marginals
of
\omega
.

By
[
33
, Theorem 4]
or
[
37
, Proposition 5.2]
, the total variation distance between
\varphi
and
\psi
is

\mathsf{tv}(\varphi,\psi)=\inf\left\{\sum_{x\neq x^{\prime}\in X}\omega(x,x^{\prime})\mid\omega\in\mathfrak{C}(\varphi,\psi)\right\}.

(18)

Proof of
Lemma

F.1
.

For any finite number of coefficients
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\in[0,1]
with
\sum_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}p_{j}=1
, we use the notation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}p_{j}\cdot\varphi_{j}
for the convex combination of distributions
\varphi_{j}
.

We need two simple facts.

Fact 1.
Given two composable matrices
A\in\mathsf{FStoch}(n,m)
and
A^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FStoch}(m,\ell)
, the
i
th column of
A;A^{\prime}
is a convex combination of the columns of
A^{\prime}
given by

(A;A^{\prime})_{i}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}A_{ij}\cdot A^{\prime}_{j},

where
A_{ij}
denotes the
j
th entry of the
i
th column. This is readily verified by direct computation (recall that
A;A^{\prime}
is the multiplied matrices
A^{\prime}A
).

Fact 2.
The total variation distance is a convex function, namely, for any
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\in[0,1]
with
\sum_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}p_{j}=1
, and
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m},\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{m},\in\mathscr{D}\underline{\mathbf{\ell}}
,

\mathsf{tv}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}p_{j}\cdot\varphi_{j},\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}p_{j}\cdot\psi_{j})\leq\sum_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}p_{j}\mathsf{tv}(\varphi_{j},\psi_{j}).

(19)

This holds because
\mathsf{tv}
is an instance of a Kantorovich metric, and those are always convex (see e.g.
[
39
, Lemma 2.6.(4)]
).

We are ready to prove that the map
{;}\colon\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{tv}}(n,m)\otimes\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{tv}}(m,\ell)\rightarrow\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{tv}}(n,\ell)
is nonexpansive. We use the first fact to express the distance between composites as a maximum of distances between convex combinations:

\displaystyle\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A;A^{\prime},B;B^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in\underline{\mathbf{n}}}\mathsf{tv}((A;A^{\prime})_{i},(B;B^{\prime})_{i})

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in\underline{\mathbf{n}}}\mathsf{tv}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}A_{ij}\cdot A^{\prime}_{j},\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}B_{ij^{\prime}}\cdot B^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}).

Then, for every
i\in\underline{\mathbf{n}}
, if we pick
\omega_{i}\in\mathfrak{C}(A_{i},B_{i})
, we have the following derivation:

\displaystyle\mathsf{tv}(

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}A_{ij}\cdot A^{\prime}_{j},\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}B_{ij^{\prime}}\cdot B^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{tv}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j,j^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}\omega_{i}(j,j^{\prime})\cdot A^{\prime}_{j},\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{589827}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{412879}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{294913}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}\vphantom{\sum}}}\displaylimits_{j,j^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}\omega_{i}(j,j^{\prime})\cdot B^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}\omega_{i}(j,j^{\prime})\mathsf{tv}(A^{\prime}_{j},B^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}).

The equality holds because
\omega_{i}
is a coupling of
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
, and the inequality holds by the second fact. We decompose the last sum in two parts, one where
j\neq j^{\prime}
and one where
j=j^{\prime}
:

\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}\omega_{i}(j,j^{\prime})\mathsf{tv}(A^{\prime}_{j},B^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})+\sum_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}\omega_{i}(j,j)\mathsf{tv}(A^{\prime}_{j},B^{\prime}_{j}).

We can loosely bound the first part because the total variation distance
\mathsf{tv}(A^{\prime}_{j},B^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})
is never bigger than
1
. Hence, writing
\Delta^{\!\mathsf{c}}_{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}=\{(j,j^{\prime})\mid j\neq j^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}\}
,

\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}\omega_{i}(j,j^{\prime})\mathsf{tv}(A^{\prime}_{j},B^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})\leq\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}\omega_{i}(j,j^{\prime})=\omega_{i}(\Delta^{\!\mathsf{c}}_{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}).

We can also bound the second part because
\mathsf{tv}(A^{\prime}_{j},B^{\prime}_{j})\leq\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
for every
j
by definition, and because the total weight of
\omega_{i}
on
\Delta_{\underline{\mathbf{m}}}=\{(j,j)\mid j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}\}
is at most
1
:

\sum_{j\in\underline{\mathbf{m}}}\omega_{i}(j,j)\mathsf{tv}(A^{\prime}_{j},B^{\prime}_{j})\leq\omega_{i}(\Delta_{\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}).

To summarise, we have shown that

\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A;A^{\prime},B;B^{\prime})\leq\max_{i\in\underline{\mathbf{n}}}\omega_{i}(\Delta^{\!\mathsf{c}}_{\underline{\mathbf{m}}})+\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}),

for any choice of couplings
\omega_{i}
.
We can conclude by using the equivalent definition of
\mathsf{tv}

18
: for any
i
and choice of
\omega_{i}
,

\omega_{i}(\Delta^{\!\mathsf{c}}_{\underline{\mathbf{m}}})\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eqn:tvcoupling}}{\leq}\mathsf{tv}(A_{i},B_{i})\overset{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eqn:tvmax}}{\leq}\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A,B).

Therefore, we have the desired inequation

\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A;A^{\prime},B;B^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A,B)+\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}).\qed

Proof of
Lemma

F.2
.

Notice that the distance between two columns of a stochastic matrix, seen as distributions, is invariant when adding rows of zeroes. This can easily be inferred from another equivalent formulation of
\mathsf{tv}
in e.g.
[
33
]
:

\mathsf{tv}(\varphi,\psi)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in X}|\varphi(x)-\psi(x)|.

(20)

If we add (or remove) an element
x\in X
which is assigned weight
0
by both
\varphi
and
\psi
, then the distance between the resulting distributions is clearly the same. In particular, the distances between the columns of the direct sums
A\oplus A^{\prime}
and
B\oplus B^{\prime}
is the distance between the columns of
A
and
B
or
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
. Formally,

\mathsf{tv}((A\oplus A^{\prime})_{i},(B\oplus B^{\prime})_{i})=\begin{cases}\mathsf{tv}(A_{i},B_{i})&i\leq n\\
\mathsf{tv}(A^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i})&i>n\end{cases}.

It readily follows that

\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A\oplus A^{\prime},B\oplus B^{\prime})=\max\{\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A,B),\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\}.

(21)

This implies the desired inequation because the maximum of two positive numbers is always smaller than their sum.
∎

\displaystyle=[]\oplus\begin{bmatrix}\scriptstyle 1-\lambda\\
\scriptstyle 1-(1-\lambda)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\scriptstyle 0\\
\scriptstyle 1-\lambda\\
\scriptstyle\lambda\end{bmatrix}.

Viewing these column vectors as distributions on
\underline{\mathbf{3}}
, the total variation distance between them is
\lambda
(compute it with
20
), so
TV
is true in
F
.
∎

This leads to the following result.

Lemma F.4
.

The functor
F\colon\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Conv}}\to\mathsf{FStoch}
is an enriched functor
F\colon\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Lib}}\to\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{tv}}
.

Proof of
Lemma

F.4
.

We know that
F
is a model of
\mathsf{Conv}
, so we want to apply
Theorem

23
. In order to do so, we recall that
\textnormal{{Seq}}_{\oplus}
,
Symm
, and
\textnormal{{Par}}_{\sqcap}
are valid in
\mathsf{FStoch}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{tv}}
. It remains to verify that
F
satisfies all the quantitative equations in
\mathsf{Lib}
, which is what
Lemma

42
achieves.

We conclude that
F
is enriched, in particular, for any
s,t\in\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Lib}}(n,m)
,

\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(F(s),F(t))\leq d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(s,t).\qed

(22)

Proof of
Lemma

44
.

Given two morphisms
f,g\colon 1\rightarrow m
, let their corresponding distributions be
\varphi,\psi\in\mathscr{D}\underline{\mathbf{m}}
. We will show that
d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(f,g)\leq\mathsf{tv}(\varphi,\psi)=\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(F(f),F(g))
. First, letting
\lambda\coloneqq\mathsf{tv}(\varphi,\psi)
,
Lemma

43
tells us that the distributions corresponding to
f
and
g
are equal to the distributions corresponding respectively to

Since
F
is fully faithful (by
Proposition

34
), it means the following equalities between diagrams can be proven in
\mathsf{Conv}
.

\varphi
=
\varphi^{\prime}
\tau
\lambda

\psi
=
\psi^{\prime}
\tau
1-\lambda

It is straightforward then to apply
3
, the thick versions of unitality and associativity, and finally
Refl
to prove the following in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Lib}}
.

\varphi
=_{0}
\varphi^{\prime}
\tau
\lambda
\psi^{\prime}

(23)

\psi
=_{0}
\varphi^{\prime}
\tau
\psi^{\prime}
1-\lambda

(24)

The only thing that differs in the right-hand sides of
23
and
24
is the left part. More precisely, we recognise both sides of
TV
. Therefore, we have

We conclude that
d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(f,g)\leq\lambda=\mathsf{tv}(\varphi,\psi)
. The converse inequality holds because
F
is an enriched functor, hence
F
is an isometry on the hom-sets with domain
1
. We know it is bijective from
Proposition

34
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

45
.

We already know that
F
is an isomorphism on the underlying categories by
Proposition

34
. Moreover, by
Lemma

F.4
,
F
is also an enriched functor, so for any
f,g\in\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Lib}}(n,m)
, we already know that
d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(f,g)\geq\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(F(f),F(g))
. Therefore, we only need to prove the converse inequation to conclude that
F
is an isomorphism of enriched categories thanks to
Corollary

B.9
.

We use
Lemma

36
to find the following decompositions of
f
and
g
:

f=(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{n});p_{m}^{n}\text{ and }g=(g_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes g_{n});p_{m}^{n},

where
f_{i}\colon 1\rightarrow m
and
F(f_{i})
is the
i
th column of
F(f)
, and similarly for
g
. Since
\textnormal{{Seq}}_{\oplus}
and
\textnormal{{Par}}_{\sqcap}
are valid in
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Lib}}
, we have

\displaystyle d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(f,g)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i\in\underline{\mathbf{n}}}d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(f_{i},g_{i})+d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(p_{m}^{n},p_{m}^{n})

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in\underline{\mathbf{n}}}d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(f_{i},g_{i}).

Since the
f_{i}
s and
g_{i}
s are morphisms with domain
1
, we can use
Lemma

44
to find that

d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(f_{i},g_{i})=\mathsf{tv}(F(f_{i}),F(g_{i})).

Thus, we conclude, as desired, that

d^{\mathsf{Lib}}(f,g)\leq\max_{i\in\underline{\mathbf{n}}}\mathsf{tv}(F(f_{i}),F(g_{i}))=\mathsf{tv}_{\times}(F(f),F(g)).\qed

G
Proofs of
Section

6

We recall the recursive definition of a cartesian term over a signature
\Sigma
whose operations all have coarity
1
. Any variable
x
(taken from a fixed countable set) is a cartesian term, and for any cartesian terms
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
and
n
-ary operation
o:n\in\Sigma
,
o(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
is a cartesian term.

Since we are working with unconditional quantitative algebraic theories, we are interested in a simpler logic than the quantitative equational logic in
[
52
]
. We take inspiration from the simpler rules in
[
65
, Figure 3.1]
, but we add back the requirement that operations are nonexpansive with a corresponding rule. Another related logic is fuzzy equational logic in
[
10
, p.143]
. In
Fig.

2
, we present the rules of quantitative equational logic.

Bot’

s=_{\bot}t

s=_{\varepsilon}t

\varepsilon^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\varepsilon

Mon’

s=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}t

\forall i\in I,s=_{\varepsilon_{i}}t

Cont’

s=_{\bigsqcup_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}t

s=t
is provable in equational logic

Refl’

s=_{\top}t

s=_{\varepsilon}t

Symm’
1
1

1

This rule is considered only when working with
{V}\mathsf{PMet}
instead of
{V}\mathsf{HMet}
.

t=_{\varepsilon}s

t=_{\varepsilon}t^{\prime}

t^{\prime}=_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}t^{\prime\prime}

Triang

t=_{\varepsilon\oplus\varepsilon^{\prime}}t^{\prime\prime}

t=_{\varepsilon}t^{\prime}

SubQ

t[s_{i}/x_{i}]=_{\varepsilon}t^{\prime}[s_{i}/x_{i}]

o:n\in\Sigma

\forall i,s_{i}=_{\varepsilon_{i}}t_{i}

NExp

o(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})=_{\sqcap_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}o(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})

Figure 2:
Rules of unconditional quantitative equational logic. The symbols
s
,
t
, and variants are universally quantified over cartesian terms, while the symbol
\varepsilon
and variants are universally quantified over
V
. The notation
t[s_{i}/x_{i}]
indicates the substitution of all occurrences of
x_{i}
with the term
s_{i}
inside
t
for all
i
.

Given an unconditional quantitative algebraic theory
\mathcal{U}=(\Sigma,E,E_{q})
, we write
\mathcal{U}\vdash s=_{\varepsilon}t
, or say that
s=_{\varepsilon}t
in
\mathcal{U}
, if there is proof tree of finite height (possibly infinite branching) that uses the rules in
Fig.

2
or the axioms in
E
and
E_{q}
and concludes
s=_{\varepsilon}t
.

With
Definition

46
, the above completes the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}
, so we can start rigorously proving things about it. We restate the definition of
d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}
for reference:

d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(\langle f_{i}\rangle,\langle g_{i}\rangle)=\sqcap_{i}\bigsqcup\left\{\varepsilon\mid\mathcal{U}\vdash f_{i}=_{\varepsilon}g_{i}\right\}.

(25)

Lemma G.1
.

The inference rule
\textnormal{{Seq}}_{\oplus}
is valid in
\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}
.

Proof.

We need to show that for all morphisms
s,t\colon n\rightarrow m
and
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\colon m\rightarrow\ell
,

d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s,t)\oplus d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\sqsubseteq d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s;s^{\prime},t;t^{\prime}).

(26)

Unrolling the definition of
d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}

25
and of composition in
\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}
, we rewrite the R.H.S. as

\sqcap_{i}\bigsqcup\left\{\varepsilon\mid\mathcal{U}\vdash s_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]=_{\varepsilon}t_{i}[t^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]\right\}.

Our goal then is to show that for any
i
, we have

\mathcal{U}\vdash s_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]=_{d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s,t)\oplus d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}t_{i}[t^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}].

We decompose it in two proofs that yield,

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle\vdash s_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]=_{d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s,t)}t_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}],\text{ and}

(27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle\vdash t_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]=_{d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}t_{i}[t^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}].

(28)

then conclude by the triangle inequality rule
Triang
.

For
27
, for any
\varepsilon
such that
\mathcal{U}\vdash s_{i}=_{\varepsilon}t_{i}
, we can use the substitution rule
SubQ
to get
\mathcal{U}\vdash s_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]=_{\varepsilon}t_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]
. Therefore, using the continuity rule
Cont’
, we also have
\mathcal{U}\vdash s_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]=_{\bigsqcup\left\{\varepsilon\mid s_{i}=_{\varepsilon}t_{i}\right\}}t_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]
. We obtain
27
by the monotonicity rule
Mon’
because

\bigsqcup\left\{\varepsilon\mid\mathcal{U}\vdash s_{i}=_{\varepsilon}t_{i}\right\}\sqsupseteq\sqcap_{k}\bigsqcup\left\{\varepsilon\mid\mathcal{U}\vdash s_{k}=_{\varepsilon}t_{k}\right\}=d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s,t).

For
28
, we recall that by the IJD property of
V
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sqcap_{j}\bigsqcup\left\{\varepsilon\mid\mathcal{U}\vdash s^{\prime}_{j}=_{\varepsilon}t^{\prime}_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup\left\{\sqcap_{j}\varepsilon_{j}\mid\forall j,\mathcal{U}\vdash s^{\prime}_{j}=_{\varepsilon_{j}}t^{\prime}_{j}\right\}.

Then, for all choices of
\varepsilon_{j}
, such that
\mathcal{U}\vdash s^{\prime}_{j}=_{\varepsilon_{j}}t^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j
, we will prove by induction on the structure of
t_{i}
that
\mathcal{U}\vdash t_{i}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]=_{\sqcap_{j}\varepsilon_{j}}t_{i}[t^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]
, and
28
will follow by
Cont
.

In the base case, we suppose that
t_{i}=x_{k}
, and we have to show
\mathcal{U}\vdash s^{\prime}_{k}=_{\sqcap_{j}\varepsilon_{j}}t^{\prime}_{k}
. This holds by using
Mon’
because by hypothesis we have
\mathcal{U}\vdash s^{\prime}_{k}=_{\varepsilon_{k}}t^{\prime}_{k}
and
\varepsilon_{k}\sqsupseteq\sqcap_{j}\varepsilon_{j}
.

For the inductive case, we suppose that
t_{i}=o(u_{1},\dots,u_{p})
and that
\mathcal{U}\vdash u_{q}[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]=_{\sqcap_{j}\varepsilon_{j}}u_{q}[t^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]
for all
q
. Then, by the nonexpansiveness rule
NExp
(and the fact that
\sqcap
is idempotent), we have
\mathcal{U}\vdash o(u_{1},\dots,u_{p})[s^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]=_{\sqcap_{j}\varepsilon_{j}}o(u_{1},\dots,u_{p})[t^{\prime}_{j}/x_{j}]
as desired.
∎

Lemma G.2
.

The inference rule
\textnormal{{Par}}_{\sqcap}
is valid in
\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}
.

Proof.

We need to show that for all morphisms
s,t\colon n\rightarrow m
and
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\colon m\rightarrow\ell
,

d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s,t)\sqcap d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\sqsubseteq d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(s\otimes s^{\prime},t\otimes t^{\prime}).

(29)

This is relatively simple because the definition of
\otimes
in
d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}
concatenates tuples. Hence, the inequation above is in fact an equation because
\sqcap
is associative.
∎

Before moving on to the proof of this section’s main theorem, we clarify the definition of
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}

Definition

48
. How exactly are the (quantitative) equations in
E
and
E_{q}
between cartesian terms ported to
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. By
[
13
, Theorem 6.1]
, there is an isomorphism
\Phi\colon\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma^{\prime},E^{c}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma}
, so that any cartesian term
t
is assigned a string diagram
\Phi^{-1}(t)
, and also a monoidal
\Sigma^{\prime}
-term after choosing a representative. Thus, if
s=t\in E
, the equation we include in
E^{\prime}
is between representatives of
\Phi^{-1}(s)
and
\Phi^{-1}(t)
, and similarly for quantitative equations in
E_{q}
. In the following, we will abusively omit the step of choosing a representative and write, e.g.,

E^{\prime}_{q}=\{\Phi^{-1}(s)=_{\varepsilon}\Phi^{-1}(t)\mid s=_{\varepsilon}t\in E_{q}\}.

Proof of
Theorem

49
.

By
Corollary

B.9
we know it is enough to have an isomorphism of the underlying categories which is locally an isometry. As stated above,
[
13
, Theorem 6.1]
gives us an isomorphism that we denote with
\Phi\colon\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma^{\prime},E^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}
.
2
2

2

We abuse notation and use the same letter
\Phi
because, as spelled out in
[
13
, Lemma 6.5]
, both functors morally act in the same way.
We have left to prove that for any natural numbers
n
and
m
,
\Phi
is locally an isometry.

Our first step is to consider
\Phi
as a model of
(\Sigma^{\prime},E^{\prime})
valued in
\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}
(recall that its underlying category is
\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\Sigma,E}
), where
\textnormal{{Seq}}_{\oplus}
and
\textnormal{{Par}}_{\sqcap}
are valid by the previous lemmas. Hence, if we can show that the equations in
E^{\prime}_{q}
are true in this model,
Theorem

23
will imply that
\Phi
is locally nonexpansive.

A quantitative equation in
E^{\prime}_{q}
has the shape
\Phi^{-1}(s)=_{\varepsilon}\Phi^{-1}(t)
for some cartesian terms
s
and
t
. To show it is true in the model
\Phi
, we need to show that
\varepsilon\sqsubseteq d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(s)),\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(t)))
. This is true because
\Phi^{-1}\circ\Phi=\mathrm{id}
and, by hypothesis,
\mathcal{U}\vdash s=_{\varepsilon}t
as that equation belongs to the axioms in
E_{q}
. We conclude by
Theorem

23
that
\Phi:\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}
is locally nonexpansive.

We still need to show that
\forall f,g\colon n\rightarrow m\in\mathcal{S}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}
,

d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(\Phi f,\Phi g)\sqsubseteq d_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(f,g).

(30)

Note that for each
i
, the
i
th element of the tuple
\Phi f
is represented by a diagram
f_{i}
(apply
\Phi^{-1}
) such that
f=\bigoplus_{i}f_{i}
. Moreover, by
\textnormal{{Par}}_{\sqcap}
, we have that
\sqcap_{i}d_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(f_{i},g_{i})\sqsubseteq d_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(f,g)
. Therefore, by
25
, it suffices to show that
d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}(\Phi f_{i},\Phi g_{i})\sqsubseteq d_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(f_{i},g_{i})
for every
i
to conclude
30
.

Since
d_{\mathcal{L}_{\scriptstyle\mathcal{U}}}
and
d_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}
are both defined as the join of derivable distances, we can prove that inequation by showing that for each
\varepsilon
such that
\mathcal{U}\vdash\Phi f_{i}=_{\varepsilon}\Phi g_{i}
,
f_{i}=_{\varepsilon}g_{i}\in E_{q}^{\mathsf{H}\oplus\sqcap}
.

We proceed by structural induction on the proof witnessing
\mathcal{U}\vdash\Phi f_{i}=_{\varepsilon}\Phi g_{i}
. For the axioms in
E_{q}
, we know that
f_{i}=_{\varepsilon}g_{i}
is an axiom in
E^{\prime}_{q}
by constructions. Most of the other rules in
Fig.

2
are directly replicated in the inference rules used to define the closure of
E^{\prime}_{q}
.
The two complicated rules are
SubQ
and
NExp
. For
SubQ
, we note that the substitution
t[\sigma(x_{i})/x_{i}]
corresponds to the composition

\Phi^{-1}(\langle\sigma(x_{1}),\dots,\sigma(x_{n})\rangle);\Phi^{-1}t,

and similarly the substitution
t^{\prime}[\sigma(x_{i})/x_{i}]
corresponds to

\Phi^{-1}(\langle\sigma(x_{1}),\dots,\sigma(x_{n})\rangle);\Phi^{-1}t^{\prime}.

Therefore, we can apply
Refl
to obtain

\Phi^{-1}(\langle\sigma(x_{1}),\dots,\sigma(x_{n})\rangle)=_{\top}\Phi^{-1}(\langle\sigma(x_{1}),\dots,\sigma(x_{n})\rangle),

the induction hypothesis to obtain
\Phi^{-1}t=_{\varepsilon}\Phi^{-1}t^{\prime}
, and finally
\textnormal{{Seq}}_{\oplus}
, to obtain the desired
\Phi^{-1}t[\sigma(x_{i})/x_{i}]=_{\varepsilon}t^{\prime}[\sigma(x_{i})/x_{i}]
.

For
NExp
,
o(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
again corresponds to a composition
